[0] h5: Report GitHub Issue

[1] p: Content selection saved. Describe the issue below:

[2] h1: Sharp Convergence Rates for Masked Diffusion Models

[3] h6: Abstract

[4] p: Discrete diffusion models have achieved strong empirical performance in text and other symbolic domains, with masked (absorbing-rate) variants emerging as competitive alternatives to autoregressive models. Among existing samplers, the Euler method remains the standard choice in many applications, and more recently, the First-Hitting Sampler (FHS) has shown considerable promise for masked diffusion models. Despite their practical success, the theoretical understanding of these samplers remains limited. Existing analyses are conducted in Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence, which often yields loose parameter dependencies and requires strong assumptions on score estimation. Moreover, these guarantees do not cover recently developed high-performance sampler of FHS. In this work, we first develop a direct total-variation (TV)–based analysis for the Euler method that overcomes these limitations. Our results relax assumptions on score estimation, improve parameter dependencies, and establish convergence guarantees without requiring any surrogate initialization. Also for this setting, we provide the first convergence lower bound for the Euler sampler, establishing tightness with respect to both the data dimension d d and the target accuracy ε \varepsilon . Finally, we analyze the FHS sampler and show that it incurs no sampling error beyond that induced by score estimation, which we show to be tight with a matching lower error bound. Overall, our analysis introduces a direct TV-based error decomposition along the CTMC trajectory and a decoupling-based path-wise analysis for FHS, which may be of independent interest.

[5] h6: Contents

[6] h2: 1 Introduction

[7] p: Diffusion models have become one of the central paradigms of generative modeling, offering state-of-the-art performance across a wide range of domains, including image synthesis [ 1 ] , video generation [ 2 ] , audio generation [ 3 ] , and text modeling [ 4 , 5 ] . The core idea is to define a forward noising process that gradually perturbs data into a tractable distribution, together with a reverse denoising process that reconstructs samples from noise.

[8] p: Recently, for applications in discrete and symbolic domains such as language, graphs, and molecules [ 6 , 7 ] , discrete-state diffusion models have demonstrated advantages over their continuous counterparts, achieving strong perplexity and negative log-likelihood performance on language modeling benchmarks [ 4 , 5 , 8 , 9 ] . In the discrete setting, the forward noising process is typically formulated as a continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC) governed by a structured rate matrix [ 10 ] . Two rate matrices are most commonly used: a uniform rate matrix, whose stationary distribution is uniform over the state space, and an absorbing rate matrix, whose stationary distribution concentrates on a fully masked absorbing state. The absorbing-rate diffusion models, often referred to as masked diffusion models (MDMs), are particularly appealing due to its close connections to established architectures such as BERT [ 11 ] and conditional masked language models (CMLM) [ 12 ] . Empirically, MDMs have achieved competitive performance relative to autoregressive models on language modeling tasks [ 8 ] .

[9] figure: Table 1: Summary of results for absorbing-rate (masked) discrete diffusion samplers in terms of the number of steps needed to achieve ε \sqrt{\varepsilon} -accuracy in total-variation. Algorithm Estimation Error Num of steps Reference τ \tau -leaping Score Entropy 𝒪 ~ ​ ( d ​ log ⁡ M ε ) \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d\log M}{\varepsilon}\right) [ 13 , Thm 2] Euler Score Entropy 𝒪 ~ ​ ( d 2 ​ M 2 ε 3 / 4 ) † \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d^{2}M^{2}}{\varepsilon^{3/4}}\right)^{\dagger} [ 14 , Thm 4.1] DMPM Score Entropy 𝒪 ~ ​ ( d ε 2 ) \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right) [ 15 , Thm 3.2.3] Euler L 1 L_{1} 𝒪 ~ ​ ( d ε 1 / 2 ) \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\right) [This paper, Thm 2 ] FHS Score Entropy Exactly d d [This paper, Thm 4 ] Comparison: Our result for the Euler method achieves the best dependence on ε \varepsilon among τ \tau -leaping type algorithms while retaining small dependencies on d d and being M M -free. Moreover, we provide the first sampling guarantee for FHS, which attains the same sampling error as other methods but using exactly d d steps. Notes: ( i ) (i) Only deterministic-step-size samplers are included in the table, because uniformization [ 16 ] yields random (potentially infinite) converging steps. ( i ​ i ) (ii) Here, d d denotes the dimension (e.g., length of the generated sentence), M M denotes the upper bound on the score estimates. The dependence on the vocabulary size S S is not explicitly shown. ( i ​ i ​ i ) (iii) In Proposition 1 , we show that our L 1 L_{1} estimation error is more relaxed than score-entropy. ( i ​ v ) (iv) While [ 14 ] does not show the dependence on M M in the main text, the dependence can be straightforwardly derived from their proof.

[10] p: Despite these empirical advances, the theoretical understanding of discrete diffusion models, especially MDMs, remains limited. Existing works using deterministic-step-size samplers have largely relied on convergence analyses in terms of Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence, most under the uniform-rate matrix [ 16 , 17 , 18 , 19 , 20 ] , and, more recently, some under the absorbing-rate [ 13 , 14 , 15 ] . However, there are currently no lower bounds showing whether existing KL-based guarantees for discrete diffusion are tight. In continuous diffusion, prior work [ 21 ] often finds that TV analyses yield sharper bounds and more accurate parameter dependence such as the dimension d d . This motivates a TV-based analysis for discrete diffusion to obtain tighter guarantees that potentially admit matching lower bounds. Doing so is nontrivial, since KL-specific tools such as change-of-measure arguments [ 19 ] do not extend to TV, and the discontinuity of the underlying space [ 20 ] introduces additional technical challenges. Furthermore, these KL-based guarantees often require strong assumptions on the estimated score function. For example, all of [ 16 , 17 , 19 , 20 ] require the assumption of a bounded score estimate, which needs typically be achieved through an additional clipping step during training [ 17 ] . These limitations give rise to our first central question.

[11] p: Question 1: Can we establish convergence guarantees with tighter parameter dependencies via a direct analysis in TV distance? Moreover, can we derive matching lower bounds that complement the existing upper bounds?

[12] p: More recently, the First-Hitting Sampler (FHS) has been shown to be highly effective for generation with MDMs [ 22 ] . Unlike classical deterministic-step-size samplers, such as Euler methods, whose number of steps must grow unboundedly to achieve vanishing TV distance, FHS can produce a high-quality sample in exactly d d steps, where d d denotes the data dimension. Despite its empirical success, the theoretical error bound of FHS has not been explored. Given its different design philosophy, the analysis of FHS requires new techniques that explicitly exploit its underlying structure. This leads us to the next question below.

[13] p: Question 2. Given a d d -step sampling procedure like FHS, how to characterize the sampling error of the algorithm? Moreover, can we also derive a matching lower bound for FHS?

[14] p: In this work, we address both questions and give affirmative answers to both of them.

[15] h3: 1.1 Our Contributions

[16] p: The central contributions of this work are (i) a new direct TV analysis tailored to the Euler method for masked diffusion models with a matching lower bound, and (ii) a novel analysis for the FHS algorithm, leading to tight error bound that matches our lower bound. Our detailed contributions include:

[17] p: Improved Parameter Dependencies for the Euler Method (with straightforward generalization to τ \tau -leaping type samplers): For the Euler method for the absorbing-rate (masked) discrete diffusion models, we strengthen existing theoretical guarantees with an improved ε \varepsilon dependence while retaining the best-known dependencies on d d and S S . Note that the same guarantees can be straightforwardly extended to vanilla τ \tau -leaping samplers using the technique in [ 20 ] . In particular, we show that, in order to reach a slightly perturbed target distribution, the required number of steps is 𝒪 ~ ​ ( d ​ S / ε ) \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(dS/\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right) . Compared to prior results, ours yields an improvement by a factor of 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) \mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\varepsilon}) . Notably, our analysis removes the need for bounded score assumptions and removes the need for a surrogate initialization typically required in previous works.

[18] p: Tight Lower Bound for the Euler Method: We provide, to our knowledge, the first lower-bound analysis for absorbing-rate discrete diffusion models using the Euler method in TV distance. Combined with our improved upper-bound, we show that our bound is tight in both the data dimension d d and the target accuracy ε \varepsilon with constant step-sizes and without early-stopping. Our result identifies the intrinsic limits of convergence when the Euler method is used.

[19] p: Error Analysis for FHS and Tight Lower Bound: We perform a rigorous analysis for the First-Hitting Sampler (FHS) [ 22 ] for masked diffusion models, which generates a sample in exactly d d steps. Specifically, we show that FHS incurs no sampling error beyond that arising from score estimation itself. In particular, the resulting error bound is independent of both the data dimension d d and the vocabulary size S S . We further show that this bound is tight, by constructing a worst-case instance for which the FHS output error matches exactly the upper bound of the score estimation error. Our result further highlights the advantages of masked diffusion models, for which efficient finite-step sampling with dimension-free guarantees is possible. Specifically, we develop a novel error analysis that decomposes the total sampling error into token-wise components. Our key insight is that FHS exploits an intrinsic decoupling structure in MDMs: the transition time τ \tau and transition index i i can be sampled exactly from the true reverse process, regardless of the accuracy of score estimation. Consequently, the overall sampling error depends solely on the estimation error incurred at each predicted token, and is therefore independent of d d and S S .

[20] h3: 1.2 Related Works

[21] p: See Appendix A for a full list of related works. As follows we only include a brief survey of the most relevant works.

[22] p: Theory on Uniform-Rate Discrete Diffusion Models. Considerable effort has been devoted to improving convergence guarantees for uniform-rate diffusion models. Unlike continuous diffusion, the resulting guarantees in the discrete setting crucially depend on the choice of sampler. Early analyses focused on uniformization-based methods [ 16 , 19 ] , exact per-step samplers [ 17 ] , and τ \tau -leaping schemes [ 10 , 19 ] . More recently, theoretical guarantees have been established for DMPM [ 18 , 15 ] as well as for more practical Tweedie τ \tau -leaping and Euler method [ 20 ] . Notably, all of these results are derived in terms of KL divergence, which yields suboptimal guarantees when translated to total variation distance.

[23] p: Theory on Masked (Absorbing-rate) Discrete Diffusion Models. [ 13 ] was the first to establish convergence guarantees for masked diffusion models under the τ \tau -leaping and the uniformization samplers, showing improved dimensional dependence compared to uniform-rate diffusion. [ 14 ] studied faster convergence rates using the Euler method and a specialized uniformization scheme, the Mask-Aware Truncated Uniformization (MATU), which eliminates the need for a bounded score assumption. In parallel, [ 15 ] derived improved dimension-dependent rates for the DMPM sampler, also without assuming bounded score.

[24] p: Comparison with concurrent work [ 23 ] . After we submitted this paper to a conference, a concurrent work [ 23 ] was recently posted on arXiv, which also studies convergence guarantees for absorbing-rate discrete diffusion models albeit under a modified truncated τ \tau -leaping sampler (in addition to uniform-rate models, which is not the focus of our paper). The two papers differ in several important ways. (i) The two works make orthogonal improvements over prior convergence results for masked diffusion models. [ 23 ] derives a convergence rate of 𝒪 ⁡ ( 𝒟 / ϵ ) \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{D}/\epsilon) under the KL divergence, where 𝒟 ≤ d ​ log ⁡ S \mathcal{D}\leq d\log S is a distribution-dependent quantity, improving previous bounds in the dependence on S S and potentially d d . In contrast, our analysis focuses on Euler discretization under the total variation (TV) metric for general distributions, improving prior results by a factor of 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / ϵ ) \mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\epsilon}) . Moreover, the TV metric allows us to start from the all-mask singleton distribution (as is typical in practice) without resorting to a surrogate initialization. (ii) We establish the first lower bound for masked diffusion models, which matches our upper bound in its dependence on d d and ϵ \epsilon , whereas [ 23 ] does not provide a lower bound for masked diffusion models. (iii) We also analyze the recently introduced, highly efficient masked diffusion sampler FHS, and show that it attains provable ϵ \epsilon -accuracy with the best-known sampling complexity of finite d d steps for masked diffusion models. This sampler is not considered in [ 23 ] .

[25] h2: 2 Preliminaries of Discrete Diffusion Models

[26] p: As in standard diffusion models, discrete diffusion models comprise a forward noising process and a reverse generation process. In this section, we describe two common frameworks used to model discrete diffusion dynamics.

[27] h3: 2.1 CTMC Forward Process

[28] p: Discrete diffusion models can be modeled as continuous-time Markov chains (CTMCs) defined over the discrete data space [ S ] d [S]^{d} . Here d d is the number of tokens and each token is drawn from a vocabulary of size S S [ 10 ] . In the forward process, the initial data is denoted by x 0 ∈ [ S ] d x_{0}\in[S]^{d} , with corresponding probability mass function (p.m.f.) q 0 q_{0} . Let R t ∈ ℝ S d × S d R_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{S^{d}\times S^{d}} be the rate matrix of the forward process. For states x , y ∈ [ S ] d x,y\in[S]^{d} , R t ​ ( x , y ) R_{t}(x,y) specifies the instantaneous rate of transition from x x to y y at time t t . The conditional distribution of y y at t + Δ ​ t t+\Delta t given x x at t t satisfies

[29] table: q t + Δ ​ t | t ( y | x ) = 𝟙 { y = x } + R t ( x , y ) Δ t + o ( Δ t ) q_{t+\Delta t|t}(y|x)=\mathds{1}\left\{y=x\right\}+R_{t}(x,y)\Delta t+o(\Delta t)

[30] p: where 𝟙 ​ { ⋅ } \mathds{1}\left\{\cdot\right\} is the indicator function. For a valid CTMC, R t ​ ( x , y ) ≥ 0 R_{t}(x,y)\geq 0 whenever x ≠ y x\neq y , and ∑ y R t ​ ( x , y ) = 0 \sum_{y}R_{t}(x,y)=0 .

[31] p: To simplify computation for large S S and d d , it is common to assume that tokens evolve independently and homogeneously across dimensions [ 10 , 5 ] . Under this assumption, the forward conditional distribution factorizes as

[34] table: R t ​ ( x , y ) = { R t t ​ o ​ k ​ ( x i , y i ) , if ​ Ham ​ ( x , y ) = 1 , 0 , otherwise , R_{t}(x,y)=\begin{cases}R_{t}^{tok}(x^{i},y^{i}),&\text{if }\mathrm{Ham}\left(x,y\right)=1,\\ 0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases} (2)

Following [ 10 ] , we set R t t ​ o ​ k = β t ​ R base R_{t}^{tok}=\beta_{t}R_{\text{base}} with a noise schedule β t > 0 \beta_{t}>0 . In this paper, we focus on the constant schedule β t ≡ 1 \beta_{t}\equiv 1 , as in prior works [ 19 , 17 , 20 , 13 ] .

[36] p: In the literature of CTMC framework, the absorbing-rate (corresponding to masked diffusion model) matrix has attracted special attention [ 4 , 5 ] . Define one vocabulary word as [ MASK ] ∈ [ S ] \mathrm{[MASK]}\in[S] . The rate matrix is given by

[37] table: R base := 𝟏 S ​ e [ MASK ] ⊺ − I S , R_{\text{base}}:=\mathbf{1}_{S}e_{\mathrm{[MASK]}}^{\intercal}-I_{S}, (3)

[38] p: where e i e_{i} is a unit vector with only the i i -th element being 1. Then, q T ≈ 𝜹 [ MASK ] d q_{T}\approx\bm{\delta}_{\mathrm{[MASK]}^{d}} , a singleton at the all-mask state.

[39] h3: 2.2 CTMC Reverse Process and the Euler Method

[49] h3: 2.3 D3PM and MDM

[50] p: Apart from the continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC) framework, discrete diffusion models can also be modeled as in Discrete Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (D3PMs). In the forward process , still assuming that each dimension propagates independently as in ( 1 ), the (token-wise) transition probability is represented as

[51] table: q t | 0 i ​ ( x t i | x 0 i ) = Cat ⁡ ( Q ¯ t ⊺ ​ e x 0 i ) , q^{i}_{t|0}(x_{t}^{i}|x_{0}^{i})=\mathrm{Cat}(\bar{Q}_{t}^{\intercal}e_{x_{0}^{i}}), (8)

[52] p: where Cat ⁡ ( 𝐩 ) \mathrm{Cat}(\mathbf{p}) represents a categorical distribution with parameter 𝐩 \mathbf{p} , and Q ¯ t \bar{Q}_{t} is the transition probability matrix. Specifically, define the mask transition matrix as

[55] p: Following [ 26 , 4 ] , a so-called “bridge distribution” q s | t , 0 q_{s|t,0} for s < t s<t is particularly useful. Indeed, under the mask transition in ( 9 ), one can show that [ 27 , 22 ]

Then, given the discretized time t k t_{k} ’s and the current state x t k x_{t_{k}} , the algorithm replace the e x 0 i e_{x_{0}}^{i} above with e x ^ 0 i e_{\hat{x}_{0}}^{i} sampled from the predictive model μ θ i ​ ( x t k , t k ) \mu_{\theta}^{i}(x_{t_{k}},t_{k}) at each step.

[58] p: One typical loss function for training μ θ \mu_{\theta} is negative evidence lower bound (NELBO) of the parameterized model [ 22 ] , given as

[59] table: ℒ ∞ ( x 0 ) = ∫ 0 1 α t ′ 1 − α t 𝔼 q t | 0 ​ ( x t ∣ x 0 ) [ ∑ l : x t l = [ MASK ] e x 0 l ⊤ log μ θ l ( x t , t ) ] d t , \mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\alpha_{t}^{\prime}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\;\mathbb{E}_{q_{t|0}(x_{t}\mid x_{0})}\left[\sum_{l:\,x_{t}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}e_{x_{0}^{l}}^{\top}\log\mu_{\theta}^{l}(x_{t},t)\right]dt, (10)

[60] p: where α t ′ = d ​ α t / d ​ t = − e − t \alpha_{t}^{\prime}=\mathrm{d}\alpha_{t}/\mathrm{d}t=-e^{-t} . Interestingly, there is a close relationship [ 22 ] between the predictive model μ θ \mu_{\theta} in MDMs and the score estimator s t ​ ( y , x ) s_{t}(y,x) in the absorbing-rate CTMC:

[62] p: Indeed, with α t = e − t \alpha_{t}=e^{-t} , one can show that the forward process of such an MDM is equivalent to that of the time-homogeneous absorbing-rate CTMC. Therefore, such MDM shares the same reverse process with the absorbing-rate CTMC.

[63] p: This relationship can also be generalized from between estimators into between true values, i.e., concrete score q t ​ ( x t 1 , ⋯ , x ^ t i , ⋯ , x t d ) q t ​ ( x t 1 , ⋯ , x t i , ⋯ , x t d ) \frac{q_{t}\!\left(x_{t}^{1},\cdots,\hat{x}_{t}^{i},\cdots,x_{t}^{d}\right)}{q_{t}\!\left(x_{t}^{1},\cdots,x_{t}^{i},\cdots,x_{t}^{d}\right)} and true token-wise reverse conditional distribution q 0 ​ ( x ^ t i | x t UM ) q_{0}\!\left(\hat{x}_{t}^{i}|x_{t}^{\mathrm{UM}}\right) [ 9 ] :

[64] table: q t ​ ( x t 1 , ⋯ , x ^ t i , ⋯ , x t d ) q t ​ ( x t 1 , ⋯ , x t i , ⋯ , x t d ) = α t 1 − α t ​ q 0 i ​ ( x ^ t i | x t U ​ M ) , \frac{q_{t}\!\left(x_{t}^{1},\cdots,\hat{x}_{t}^{i},\cdots,x_{t}^{d}\right)}{q_{t}\!\left(x_{t}^{1},\cdots,x_{t}^{i},\cdots,x_{t}^{d}\right)}=\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\,q_{0}^{i}\!\left(\hat{x}_{t}^{i}|x_{t}^{UM}\right), (12)

[65] p: where x t U ​ M x_{t}^{UM} denotes the collection of unmasked coordinates. Given such a close relationship, we will use “MDMs” and “absorbing-rate diffusion models” interchangeably.

[66] h3: 2.4 First Hitting Sampler (FHS)

[67] p: First Hitting Sampler (FHS), as referenced in Algorithm 1 , is a sampling algorithm specifically designed for MDMs, which was first proposed in [ 22 ] . Instead of employing a pre-defined discretized time-grid as seen in previous samplers (e.g., τ \tau -leaping and the Euler’s method), FHS simulates the sampling process in MDMs by directly characterizing each individual unmasking event. As a result, FHS avoids unnecessary intermediate steps and requires exactly d d sampling steps to generate a complete sequence, making it a substantially more efficient sampler.

[68] p: Despite such a significant empirical improvement, there still lacks theoretical characterization of the convergence error of FHS, i.e., how score estimation error influences the convergence rate of FHS. Furthermore, it also remains unclear whether the minimizer of the training objective (NELBO) for MDMs coincides with the minimizer of the score entropy loss. To address these open questions and provide a principled understanding of FHS, we will conduct analysis on FHS in Section 4 .

[69] figure: Algorithm 1 First Hitting Sampler (FHS) [ 22 ] Input: noise schedule α t = e − t \alpha_{t}=e^{-t} with inverse α − 1 ​ ( u ) = − log ⁡ u \alpha^{-1}(u)=-\log u , pretrained masked diffusion model μ θ \mu_{\theta} 𝐱 d ← [ [ MASK ] , [ MASK ] , … , [ MASK ] ] \mathbf{x}_{d}\leftarrow[\mathrm{[MASK]},\mathrm{[MASK]},\ldots,\mathrm{[MASK]}] ; α τ d ← 0 \alpha_{\tau_{d}}\leftarrow 0 ; for n ← d n\leftarrow d to 1 1 do Sample u n ∼ Uniform ⁡ ( [ 0 , 1 ] ) u_{n}\sim\mathrm{Uniform}([0,1]) ; τ n − 1 ← α − 1 ​ ( 1 − u n 1 / n ​ ( 1 − α τ n ) ) \tau_{n-1}\leftarrow\alpha^{-1}\!\bigl(1-u_{n}^{1/n}(1-\alpha_{\tau_{n}})\bigr) ; Randomly select l l uniformly from { i : 𝐱 n ( i ) = [ MASK ] } \{\,i:\mathbf{x}_{n}^{(i)}=\mathrm{[MASK]}\,\} ; 𝐱 n − 1 ← 𝐱 n \mathbf{x}_{n-1}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}_{n} ; Sample z ∼ Cat ⁡ ( μ θ l ​ ( 𝐱 n , τ n − 1 ) ) z\sim\mathrm{Cat}\!\bigl(\mu_{\theta}^{l}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\tau_{n-1})\bigr) ; 𝐱 n − 1 ( l ) ← z \mathbf{x}_{n-1}^{(l)}\leftarrow z ; Output: 𝐱 0 \mathbf{x}_{0}

[70] h3: 2.5 Key Notations

Define Ham ⁡ ( x , y ) \mathrm{Ham}\left(x,y\right) as the Hamming distance between two vectors x x and y y . For a positive integer n n , [ n ] := { 1 , … , n } [n]:=\{1,\dots,n\} . Write 𝟏 S \bm{1}_{S} as a vector of length S S that contains all 1’s, and I S I_{S} as an identity matrix of size S × S S\times S .

[72] h2: 3 Convergence Guarantees for the Euler Method

[73] p: In this section, we present our improved upper error bounds and the first convergence lower bound for the Euler method. In particular, differently from most existing theoretical results (e.g., [ 19 , 13 ] ), our results are directly characterized by TV distance without providing an upper bound in KL divergence followed by invoking the Pinsker’s inequality. As a result, our approach yields tighter bounds on the number of steps and improves other parameter dependencies as well.

[74] h3: 3.1 General Decomposition in Total Variation

[75] p: The following theorem shows the decomposition of the total-variation error incurred by a mismatched reverse process under general reverse rates. It will later be instantiated to obtain an improved parameter dependence.

[76] h6: Theorem 1 .

[77] p: Recall that the reverse process p t p_{t} also follows a CTMC. Denote its initial distribution by p 0 p_{0} and the rate by R ^ t \hat{R}_{t} . Then,

[78] table: TV ( q → T − δ , p T − δ ) ≤ TV ( q → 0 , p 0 ) + ∑ k = 0 N − 1 ∫ t k t k + 1 𝔼 x t ∼ q → t ∑ y : y ≠ x t | R ^ t ( x t , y ) − R → t ( x t , y ) | d t . \mathrm{TV}(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle q\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle q\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle q\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle q\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle q\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle q\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle q\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{t}}\sum_{y:y\neq x_{t}}\left|\hat{R}_{t}(x_{t},y)-\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle R\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle R\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle R\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle R\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{t}(x_{t},y)\right|\mathrm{d}t.

[79] p: Theorem 1 shows that the final TV error between the marginal distributions of the true and mismatched processes can be upper-bounded by the sum of (1) the initial TV error and (2) the error accumulated along the sampling path from mismatched rate matrices. Compared with prior KL-based analyses [ 19 , 20 ] , the TV decomposition yields an accumulation term characterized not by a Bregman divergence but by the absolute difference of the rate matrices. Such a dependence on the absolute rate difference was also seen in [ 10 ] .

[80] p: The full proof of Theorem 1 is in Appendix D . Our argument starts by decomposing the TV distance into the initial error and the integral of its rate of change over time. While Reynolds’ Transport Theorem can be used to handle this rate of change in continuous spaces [ 28 , Appendix K] , it cannot be directly applied in the discrete setting due to the absence of directional vectors. To address this, we develop a discrete analogue. We first prove a result showing that the rate of change in the marginal difference for the boundary terms vanishes ( Lemma 2 ), leaving only probability derivatives. Then, these are expressed through the Kolmogorov forward equations and rearranged, yielding a bound in terms of the absolute differences between the true and mismatched rate matrices.

[81] h3: 3.2 Convergence Upper Bound for the Euler Method

[82] p: In this subsection, we characterize the convergence rate with explicit parameter dependencies for the Euler method. Our analysis is made only under the following estimation error.

[83] h6: Assumption 1 .

[84] p: The score estimation error satisfies that ℒ T ​ V ​ ( s ) ≤ ε score \mathcal{L}_{TV}(s)\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\text{score}}} , where

[85] table: ℒ T ​ V ( s ) := ∑ k = 0 N − 1 ( t k + 1 − t k ) 𝔼 x k ∼ q T − t k [ ∑ y : y ≠ x k R T − t k ( y , x k ) | s T − t k ( y , x k ) − q T − t k ​ ( y ) q T − t k ​ ( x k ) | ] . \mathcal{L}_{TV}(s):=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(t_{k+1}-t_{k})\mathbb{E}_{x_{k}\sim q_{T-t_{k}}}\bigg[\sum_{y:y\neq x_{k}}R_{T-t_{k}}(y,x_{k})\left|s_{T-t_{k}}(y,x_{k})-\frac{q_{T-t_{k}}(y)}{q_{T-t_{k}}(x_{k})}\right|\bigg].

[86] p: Here we assume that the score estimation is accurate in L 1 L_{1} (i.e., the expected sum of absolute differences between the rate matrices), a condition that naturally arises from our direct TV-based analysis. A similar criterion also appears in the TV-based analysis of [ 10 ] .

[87] p: While both Assumption 1 and [ 10 , Theorem 1] capture the estimation error in terms of the sum of absolute rate differences, there is a key difference between the two. [ 10 , Theorem 1] requires that the sum of difference is small for all x x and time t t , which results in a loose bound around t ≈ 0 t\approx 0 where the score function diverges for all x x . In comparison, Assumption 1 only requires the sum average over both x x and t t to be small.

[88] p: An alternative objective commonly used in the literature is the score entropy loss [ 5 ] , which has also been adopted in prior theoretical analyses [ 17 , 19 , 20 ] . In Proposition 1 , we connect the two estimation errors and show that our Assumption 1 is more relaxed than the score entropy error.

[89] h6: Proposition 1 .

[90] p: Recall the score entropy loss at time t t in ( 5 ). If R ^ t ​ ( x , y ) − R → t ​ ( x , y ) = o ⁡ ( 1 ) , ∀ t , x , y \hat{R}_{t}(x,y)-\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle R\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle R\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle R\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle R\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{t}(x,y)=o(1),\penalty\ \forall t,x,y and sup t ∈ ( δ , T ) ℒ S ​ E ​ ( s t , t ) ≲ ε score ′ \sup_{t\in(\delta,T)}\mathcal{L}_{SE}(s_{t};t)\lesssim\varepsilon_{\text{score}}^{\prime} , choosing T ≍ log ⁡ ( d / ε ) T\asymp\log(d/\sqrt{\varepsilon}) , δ ≍ ε score ′ d \delta\asymp\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\text{score}}^{\prime}}}{d} , and η k = κ ​ min ⁡ { 1 , T − t k } \eta_{k}=\kappa\min\left\{1,T-t_{k}\right\} , we have

[91] table: ℒ T ​ V ​ ( s ) ≲ d ​ S ⋅ ε score ′ . \mathcal{L}_{TV}(s)\lesssim\sqrt{dS\cdot\varepsilon_{\text{score}}^{\prime}}.

[92] p: The following theorem provides our improved convergence rate for the absorbing-rate Euler method.

[93] h6: Theorem 2 .

[94] p: Suppose that Assumption 1 hold. Also suppose that the number of [ MASK ] \mathrm{[MASK]} tokens in the data m ⁡ ( x 0 ) ≤ m 0 = 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) m(x_{0})\leq m_{0}=\mathcal{O}(1) almost surely. Using the absorbing-rate matrix in ( 3 ), if we initialize with p 0 = 𝛅 [ MASK ] d p_{0}=\bm{\delta}_{\mathrm{[MASK]}^{d}} , sample using the Euler method, and choose the step-sizes as η k = κ ​ min ⁡ { 1 , T − t k } \eta_{k}=\kappa\min\left\{1,T-t_{k}\right\} , then we have

[95] table: TV ⁡ ( q 0 , p T − δ ) ≲ d ​ e − T + ε score + κ ​ d ​ S ​ ( 1 − e − T + log ⁡ δ − 1 ) + d ​ δ . \mathrm{TV}(q_{0},p_{T-\delta})\lesssim de^{-T}+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\text{score}}}+\kappa dS(1-e^{-T}+\log\delta^{-1})+d\delta.

[96] p: Thus, if δ ≍ ε / d \delta\asymp\sqrt{\varepsilon}/d , T ≍ log ⁡ ( d / ε ) T\asymp\log(d/\sqrt{\varepsilon}) , and κ ≍ ε / ( d ​ S ​ log ⁡ δ − 1 ) \kappa\asymp\sqrt{\varepsilon}/(dS\log\delta^{-1}) , we achieve TV ⁡ ( q 0 , p T − δ ) ≲ ε \mathrm{TV}(q_{0},p_{T-\delta})\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon} with N = 𝒪 ~ ​ ( d ​ S / ε ) N=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(dS/\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right) sampling steps.

[97] p: Comparing to prior works, our study features the following improvements.

[98] p: (Improving parameter dependence) Theorem 2 provides the first guarantee for the absorbing discrete diffusion models to achieve 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) \mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\varepsilon}) sampling complexity. This improves the dependence of the number of steps on ε \varepsilon in [ 13 , 14 , 15 ] by a factor of 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) \mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\varepsilon}) , while the dependencies on d d and S S remain to be as small as 𝒪 ⁡ ( d ​ S ) \mathcal{O}(dS) .

[99] p: (Exact initialization) While all of [ 13 , 14 , 15 ] require the algorithm to initialize from a surrogate distribution, here we can safely initialize from 𝜹 [ MASK ] d \bm{\delta}_{\mathrm{[MASK]}^{d}} , which is the singleton at all-mask state. The underlying reason is that while KL ( q T | | 𝜹 [ MASK ] d ) = ∞ \mathrm{KL}(q_{T}||\bm{\delta}_{\mathrm{[MASK]}^{d}})=\infty , TV ⁡ ( q T , 𝜹 [ MASK ] d ) \mathrm{TV}(q_{T},\bm{\delta}_{\mathrm{[MASK]}^{d}}) does not diverge due to irregularities of singletons. Thus, building upon Theorem 1 , the convergence error can still be well-controlled even if starting from p 0 = 𝜹 [ MASK ] d p_{0}=\bm{\delta}_{\mathrm{[MASK]}^{d}} .

[100] p: (Removing boundedness assumption) Differently from [ 13 ] , we do not require that the score estimates are bounded. Such an assumption can be removed thanks to our direct TV-based analysis. In prior KL-based analyses, the score entropy term involves a logarithmic factor that becomes unstable when the estimated score is too small or too large [ 19 , 20 , 17 ] . In contrast, our TV-based analysis only depends on the absolute difference induced by score estimation, avoiding this instability and removing the need for a bounded score estimate.

[101] p: Next, we study a special case for which a non-early-stopped result can be obtained. In particular, we introduce an extra parameter γ ⁡ ( q 0 ) \gamma(q_{0}) that characterizes the likelihood of [ MASK ] \mathrm{[MASK]} in q 0 q_{0} , which first appeared in [ 13 ] . Note that in practice, γ ⁡ ( q 0 ) \gamma(q_{0}) is typically very small, reflecting the fact that only a small fraction of tokens are masked in the data (aside from a few corrupted entries).

[102] h6: Corollary 1 .

[103] p: Define γ ( q 0 ) := min i ∈ [ d ] q 0 i ​ ( [ MASK ] | x − i ) max a i ∈ [ S ] : a i ≠ [ MASK ] q 0 i ( a i | x − i ) > 0 \gamma(q_{0}):=\min_{i\in[d]}\frac{q_{0}^{i}(\mathrm{[MASK]}|x^{-i})}{\max_{a^{i}\in[S]:a^{i}\neq\mathrm{[MASK]}}q_{0}^{i}(a^{i}|x^{-i})}>0 . Then, under the same set of assumptions in Theorem 2 , and choosing constant step-sizes η k ≡ κ \eta_{k}\equiv\kappa , we have

[104] table: TV ( q 0 , p T ) ≲ d e − T + ε score + κ d S ( 1 − e − T + γ ( q 0 ) − 1 / 2 ) . \mathrm{TV}(q_{0},p_{T})\lesssim de^{-T}+\sqrt{\varepsilon_{\text{score}}}+\kappa dS(1-e^{-T}+\gamma(q_{0})^{-1/2}).

[105] p: Thus, if we let T ≍ log ⁡ ( d / ε ) T\asymp\log(d/\sqrt{\varepsilon}) and κ ≍ ε / ( d ​ S ) \kappa\asymp\sqrt{\varepsilon}/(dS) , then TV ⁡ ( q 0 , p T ) ≲ ε \mathrm{TV}(q_{0},p_{T})\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon} with N = 𝒪 ~ ​ ( d ​ S / ε ) N=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(dS/\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right) sampling steps.

[106] h3: 3.3 Convergence Lower Bound for the Euler Method

[107] p: Given the strong performance guarantees of the Euler method, an important question is whether these upper bounds are tight, as this directly implies the fundamental theoretical limits of the algorithm. As follows, we provide a convergence lower bound under the absorbing-rate using the Euler sampler, assuming no estimation error.

[108] h6: Theorem 3 .

[109] p: Suppose that the Euler method with constant step-sizes is used such that η k = κ \eta_{k}=\kappa . Then, there exists q ∗ q^{*} where γ ⁡ ( q ∗ ) > 0 \gamma(q^{*})>0 , such that in order to achieve TV ⁡ ( q ∗ , p T ) ≲ ε \mathrm{TV}(q^{*},p_{T})\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon} , the number of steps must satisfy N = Ω ~ ​ ( d / ε ) N=\tilde{\Omega}(d/\sqrt{\varepsilon}) .

[110] p: Theorem 3 is the first convergence lower bound for the Euler method, and in the discrete diffusion literature at large. It shows that in order to achieve ε \sqrt{\varepsilon} -TV error, the number of steps required for the Euler method is at least in the order of d / ε d/\sqrt{\varepsilon} (ignoring log-factors). If combined with Corollary 1 , it shows that our upper bound on the number of steps is tight in d d and ε \varepsilon (up to log-factors).

[111] p: The full proof of Theorem 3 is provided in Appendix G . The key idea is to couple the forward process starting from a singleton 𝜹 𝒂 \bm{\delta}_{\bm{a}} with another forward process that starts from q γ = : q ∗ q_{\gamma}=:q^{*} . Such a construction enables an explicit characterization of the score function and, consequently, the Euler updates at each step. Because tokens remain fixed once they are unmasked, we can derive the final element-wise mask probability p M p_{M} , which captures the key dynamics of the process. Comparing q γ q_{\gamma} with the resulting distribution then yields a lower bound on the TV distance, from which we obtain the required relationship of T T and κ \kappa as a function of ε \varepsilon , and hence the step complexity.

[112] h2: 4 Convergence Guarantees for the FHS Algorithm

[113] p: In this section, we provide our convergence analysis of FHS (see Algorithm 1 ). Our analysis will be performed under the following assumption on the score estimation error.

[114] h6: Assumption 2 .

[115] p: Recall ℒ S ​ E \mathcal{L}_{SE} from ( 5 ). Suppose that s t s_{t} satisfies that

[116] table: ∫ 0 + ∞ ℒ S ​ E ​ ( s , t ) ​ 𝑑 t ≤ ε score ′′ . \textstyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{SE}(s;t)\mathrm{d}t\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{score}}.

[117] p: In the studies using the integrated score entropy loss, the time integral is typically truncated at T ≍ log ⁡ ( d / ε ) T\asymp\log(d/\varepsilon) [ 16 , 19 ] . We instead extend the upper limit to + ∞ +\infty , for two reasons. First, for such a choice of T T , taking s t ​ ( y , x ) ≍ e − t 1 − e − t s_{t}(y,x)\asymp\frac{e^{-t}}{1-e^{-t}} yields (cf. [ 13 ] ): ∫ T + ∞ ℒ S ​ E ​ ( s , t ) ​ 𝑑 t ≲ ∫ T + ∞ 1 e t − 1 ​ 𝑑 t ≲ ε \int_{T}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{SE}(s;t)\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\int_{T}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\varepsilon . Therefore, extending the integration range to + ∞ +\infty is asymptotically equivalent, as the additional tail contributes at most 𝒪 ⁡ ( ε ) \mathcal{O}(\varepsilon) . Second, we show in the following proposition that such integrated score entropy loss is equivalent to the standard training protocol (i.e., NELBO) in ( 10 ) for masked diffusion models up to a fixed constant that depends only on the data distribution q 0 ​ ( ⋅ ) q_{0}(\cdot) .

[118] h6: Proposition 2 .

[119] p: Suppose there are no [ MASK ] \mathrm{[MASK]} tokens in the data. Then we have

[120] table: ∫ 0 + ∞ ℒ S ​ E ( s ; t ) d t = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 [ ℒ ∞ ( x 0 ) ] − ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) , \int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{SE}(s;t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})]-\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right),

[121] p: where ℳ k ⊆ [ d ] \mathcal{M}_{k}\subseteq[d] , with | ℳ k | = k |\mathcal{M}_{k}|=k for k = 1 , … , d k=1,\ldots,d , denotes the subset with exactly k k token indices uniformly sampled from the total index set, ℳ k c = [ d ] ∖ ℳ k \mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}=[d]\setminus\mathcal{M}_{k} , H ⁡ ( ⋅ ) H(\cdot) denotes the entropy, x 0 ℳ k c := { x 0 l } l ∈ ℳ k c x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}}:=\{x_{0}^{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}} , and q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}}) denotes the conditional distribution of x 0 l x_{0}^{l} given x 0 ℳ k c x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}} . Moreover, ℒ S ​ E \mathcal{L}_{SE} is defined in ( 5 ), and ℒ ∞ \mathcal{L}_{\infty} is the NELBO defined in ( 10 ). In other words, the expected NELBO 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 ​ [ ℒ ∞ ​ ( x 0 ) ] \mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})] is equivalent (up to a constant) to the integrated ℒ S ​ E \mathcal{L}_{SE} .

[122] p: Proposition 2 establishes the effectiveness of the training process for MDMs [ 27 ] , which aims to minimize 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 ​ [ ℒ ∞ ​ ( x 0 ) ] \mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})] . Further, our proof shows that

[123] table: 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 [ ℒ ∞ ( x 0 ) ] − ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) ≥ 0 , \mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})]-\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right)\geq 0,

[124] p: and the equality holds if and only if q 0 l ( ⋅ | x t U ​ M ) = μ θ l ( x t , t ) [ ⋅ ] q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{t}^{UM})=\mu_{\theta}^{l}(x_{t},t)[\cdot] for all t t , x t x_{t} , and l l , where x t U ​ M x_{t}^{UM} denotes the collection of unmasked coordinates of x t x_{t} . This implies that the right-most summation term is an attainable lower bound in the training process, which can be reached when the trained model is perfect. The full proof of Proposition 2 is in Appendix H .

[125] p: We now provide the error bound for FHS in the following theorem.

[126] h6: Theorem 4 .

[127] p: Suppose there are no [ MASK ] \mathrm{[MASK]} tokens in the data, and suppose that Assumption 2 holds. Then,

[128] table: KL ( q 0 | | ℙ FHS ) ≤ ε score ′′ . \textstyle\mathrm{KL}(q_{0}||\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}})\leq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{score}}.

[129] p: Here ℙ FHS \mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}} denotes the output distribution induced by the FHS algorithm.

[130] p: Theorem 4 shows that FHS can achieve ε \varepsilon -KL error in exactly d d sampling steps. This is the first result that shows that discrete diffusion samplers could achieve vanishing convergence error (as the training error decreases) within the finite number of steps. Notably, the resulting error bound introduces no additional sources of error beyond score estimation error and exhibits no further dependence on either the data dimension d d or the vocabulary size S S . This highlights the efficiency of the FHS algorithm compared to previous diffusion samplers, which generally require substantially more than d d steps to reach the same level of accuracy. More generally, our result is a further showcase of the advantage of choosing masked diffusion models over their uniform-rate counterpart.

[131] p: The full proof of Theorem 4 is provided in Appendix J . We highlight our main idea as follows. We start by showing in Proposition 3 the decoupling property of MDMs: the transition time τ \tau and transition index i i can be sampled exactly from true reverse process, and remain independent of the estimation error introduced by μ θ \mu_{\theta} . We then use this decoupling property to decompose the path-wise error into the error arising from predicting x 0 i x_{0}^{i} via μ θ \mu_{\theta} in each sampling step. Finally, we transform the path-wise error to resulting sampling error on x 0 x_{0} using data processing inequality.

[132] p: Continuing the error upper bound of FHS in Theorem 4 , we now investigate whether the upper bound is tight. As follows, we derive a lower bound in Theorem 5 . Specifically, since Assumption 2 allows any form of μ θ \mu_{\theta} resulting from the training procedure, our chosen μ θ \mu_{\theta} , as one particular instance, provides an error lower bound over the class of all such estimators.

[133] h6: Theorem 5 .

[134] p: There exists a pair ( q 0 , μ θ ) (q_{0},\mu_{\theta}) satisfying Assumption 2 such that

[135] table: KL ( q 0 | | ℙ FHS ) ≥ ε score ′′ . \mathrm{KL}(q_{0}||\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}})\geq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{score}}.

[136] p: Theorem 5 is the first convergence lower bound for the FHS algorithm. Specifically, it shows that the upper bound in Theorem 4 is exactly tight. The full proof is provided in Appendix K . Different from the proof of Theorem 3 , we construct both a target distribution q 0 q_{0} and a special form of the estimated predictive model μ θ \mu_{\theta} . Indeed, such a construction corresponds to a worst-case pair ( q 0 , μ θ ) (q_{0},\mu_{\theta}) , where μ θ \mu_{\theta} can possibly be obtained during the training under Assumption 2 . Such a construction allows us to explicitly characterize both the score estimation error and the final KL convergence error.

[137] h2: 5 Conclusion

[138] p: In this work, we presented a novel direct TV analysis for discrete diffusion models that yields tighter parameter dependencies than prior KL-based results. Our framework improves convergence rates, removes the need for bounded score assumptions and surrogate initialization, and establishes the first tight lower bounds in TV. Further, we showed that FHS incurs no sampling error beyond that arising from score estimation itself, without additional dependence on the system parameters. We then showed that this error bound is tight via a lower bound by a worst-case construction. Looking ahead, an interesting direction is to investigate high-order variants of FHS for further acceleration.

[141] h2: References

[142] h2: Appendix A Full Related Works

[143] p: Empirical Studies on Discrete Diffusion Models. Due to the large portion of works, we will only include those early works and those most relevant works recently. Compared to continuous-space diffusion models, discrete-space diffusion models are emerging as strong alternatives for generative tasks involving symbolic data [ 10 , 5 ] (see also surveys: [ 6 , 7 ] ). The continuous-time formulation of discrete diffusion was first introduced in [ 10 ] . More recently, [ 5 ] proposed the score-entropy estimation loss and demonstrated empirical success in text generation. They further introduced Tweedie τ \tau -leaping, a new sampler derived from an approximation of Tweedie’s formula. For per-step updates, most empirical implementations adopt categorical sampling, which has shown strong practical performance.

[144] p: More recently, an increasing number of empirical studies have focused on masked diffusion models, which is often found to deliver superior performance in text and image applications. [ 8 ] simplified the variational training objective into a weighted cross-entropy integral and introduced a state-dependent masking schedule to dynamically adjust rates for better generation quality. Building on this, [ 9 ] reparameterized the concrete score as a time-dependent scalar and a conditional distribution, leading to the Reparameterized Absorbing Discrete Diffusion (RADD) model for efficient training and sampling. Similarly, [ 27 ] exploited the absorbing state structure to derive a tighter ELBO through Rao-Blackwellization and proposed a semi-autoregressive decoding strategy for flexible sequence generation. Building on this line of work, [ 22 ] introduced the First-Hitting Sampler, which exactly samples from the continuous-time diffusion process under the assumption of a perfectly learned score. More recently, [ 29 ] developed a pre-trained model for masked diffusion models.

[145] p: Theory on Uniform-Rate Discrete Diffusion Models. In contrast to the extensive theoretical literature on continuous diffusion models, rigorous results for discrete diffusion remain relatively limited. An early contribution is [ 10 ] , which analyzed τ \tau -leaping under the total variation metric but relied on strong estimation assumptions and exhibited unfavorable parameter dependencies. Subsequent works shifted focus to controlling the score–entropy estimation error. In particular, [ 16 ] established convergence guarantees for the uniformization sampler on the d d -dimensional hypercube, later extended to general discrete spaces [ S ] d [S]^{d} in [ 19 ] . For deterministic step-size methods, [ 17 , 18 ] assumed access to an exact per-step solver, while [ 19 ] analyzed the more practical τ \tau -leaping sampler. Among these works, [ 17 ] required control of the score–entropy loss along the entire continuous sampling trajectory, whereas [ 19 , 18 ] imposed this requirement only on the discrete time grid. More recently, [ 20 ] developed an alternative analysis that avoids the Girsanov change-of-measure technique, yielding improved parameter dependencies and directly motivating the present work. Notably, all of these analyses [ 16 , 19 , 17 , 18 , 20 ] are conducted in terms of KL divergence, which leads to suboptimal bounds when converted to total variation distance. Beyond convergence analysis, [ 30 ] proposed a theoretically motivated improvement to discrete classifier-free guidance (CFG), and [ 31 ] extended discrete diffusion techniques to continuous data distributions via quantization.

[146] p: Theory on Masked (Absorbing-rate) Discrete Diffusion Models. On the theoretical side, [ 13 ] was the first to establish convergence guarantees for masked diffusion models, showing improved dimensional dependence compared to uniform-rate diffusion. [ 14 ] studied faster convergence rates using the Euler method and a specialized uniformization scheme, the Mask-Aware Truncated Uniformization (MATU), which eliminates the need for a bounded score assumption. In parallel, [ 15 ] derived improved dimension-dependent rates for the DMPM sampler, also without the need of a bounded score. From a different perspective, [ 32 , 33 ] provided theoretical guarantees for masked language models. Beyond the analysis of unconditional models, [ 34 , 30 ] examined classifier-free guidance in the context of masked diffusion models.

[147] p: Comparison with concurrent work [ 23 ] . After we submitted this paper to a conference, a concurrent work [ 23 ] was recently posted on arXiv, which also studies convergence guarantees for absorbing-rate discrete diffusion models albeit under a modified truncated τ \tau -leaping sampler (in addition to uniform-rate models, which is not the focus of our paper). The two papers differ in several important ways. (i) The two works make orthogonal improvements over prior convergence results for masked diffusion models. [ 23 ] derives a convergence rate of 𝒪 ⁡ ( 𝒟 / ϵ ) \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{D}/\epsilon) under the KL divergence, where 𝒟 ≤ d ​ log ⁡ S \mathcal{D}\leq d\log S is a distribution-dependent quantity, improving previous bounds in the dependence on S S and potentially d d . In contrast, our analysis focuses on Euler discretization under the total variation (TV) metric for general distributions, improving prior results by a factor of 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / ϵ ) \mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{\epsilon}) . Moreover, the TV metric allows us to start from the all-mask singleton distribution (as is typical in practice) without resorting to a surrogate initialization. (ii) We establish the first lower bound for masked diffusion models, which matches our upper bound in its dependence on d d and ϵ \epsilon , whereas [ 23 ] does not provide a lower bound for masked diffusion models. (iii) We also analyze the recently introduced, highly efficient masked diffusion sampler FHS, and show that it attains provable ϵ \epsilon -accuracy with the best-known sampling complexity of finite d d steps for masked diffusion models. This sampler is not considered in [ 23 ] .

[192] p: The proof is now complete.

[193] h2: Appendix E Proof of Theorem 2

[194] p: We follow the idea of [ 20 ] to analyze the Euler method by constructing a truncated version of the vanilla τ \tau -leaping sampler. In Lemma 8 of [ 20 ] , it is shown that the truncated τ \tau -leaping sampler is asymptotically equivalent to the Euler method. Also, from Lemma 7 of [ 20 ] , one important property of this sampler is that its sampling rate R ^ t \hat{R}_{t} is piecewise constant and, given x t k ∈ [ S ] d x_{t_{k}}\in[S]^{d} , we have

[195] table: R ^ t ​ ( x t k , ⋅ ) = R ^ t k ​ ( x t k , ⋅ ) . \hat{R}_{t}(x_{t_{k}},\cdot)=\hat{R}_{t_{k}}(x_{t_{k}},\cdot). (14)

[201] p: It now remains to upper-bound the initialization and the discretization error.

[202] h3: E.2 Step 2: Upper-bound initialization error

[245] p: We first construct a distribution q ∗ q^{*} as follows. Define γ := ε 1 / 4 d > 0 \gamma:=\frac{\varepsilon^{1/4}}{d}>0 . We want to define an augmented forward process such that q ∗ q^{*} is the γ \gamma -perturbation of q 0 = 𝜹 𝒂 q_{0}=\bm{\delta}_{\bm{a}} , denoted by q γ q_{\gamma} . Here we let 𝜹 𝒂 \bm{\delta}_{\bm{a}} denote a delta-distribution such that a i ≠ [ MASK ] , ∀ i ∈ [ d ] a^{i}\neq\mathrm{[MASK]},\penalty\ \forall i\in[d] . Note that such an augmentation is key to the proof, where we are coupling the forward process starting from 𝜹 𝒂 \bm{\delta}_{\bm{a}} and that starting from q γ q_{\gamma} .

The derivation of ( 24 ) implies that ∫ 0 + ∞ ℒ S ​ E ​ ( s , t ) ​ 𝑑 t = 0 \int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{SE}(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}t=0 if and only if q 0 l ( ⋅ | x t U ​ M ) = μ θ l ( x t , t ) [ ⋅ ] q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{t}^{UM})=\mu_{\theta}^{l}(x_{t},t)[\cdot] for all t t , x t x_{t} , and l l .

[285] p: First, we show that the first term in ( 24 ) satisfies:

[286] table: ∫ 0 + ∞ − α t 1 − α t 𝔼 x t ∑ l : x t l = [ MASK ] ∑ a ≠ [ MASK ] q 0 l ( a | x t U ​ M ) log μ θ l ( x t , t ) [ a ] d t = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 [ ℒ ∞ ( x 0 ) ] . \int_{0}^{+\infty}-\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{t}}\sum_{l:x_{t}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}\sum_{a\neq\mathrm{[MASK]}}q_{0}^{l}(a|x_{t}^{UM})\log\mu_{\theta}^{l}(x_{t},t)[a]\,\mathrm{d}t=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})]. (25)

[287] p: To proceed, by the definition of NELBO ℒ ∞ ​ ( x 0 ) \mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0}) in ( 10 ), we have

[288] table: 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 ​ [ ℒ ∞ ​ ( x 0 ) ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})] = ∫ 0 + ∞ − α t 1 − α t 𝔼 x t , x 0 ∑ l : x t l = [ MASK ] log μ θ l ( x t , t ) [ x 0 l ] d t \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}-\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{t},x_{0}}\sum_{l:x_{t}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}\log\mu_{\theta}^{l}(x_{t},t)[x_{0}^{l}]\,\mathrm{d}t = ∫ 0 + ∞ − α t 1 − α t 𝔼 x t 𝔼 x 0 | x t ∑ l : x t l = [ MASK ] log μ θ l ( x t , t ) [ x 0 l ] d t \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}-\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}|x_{t}}\sum_{l:x_{t}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}\log\mu_{\theta}^{l}(x_{t},t)[x_{0}^{l}]\,\mathrm{d}t = ∫ 0 + ∞ − α t 1 − α t 𝔼 x t ∑ l : x t l = [ MASK ] 𝔼 x 0 l ∼ q 0 l ( ⋅ | x t U ​ M ) log μ θ l ( x t , t ) [ x 0 l ] d t \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}-\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{t}}\sum_{l:x_{t}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}^{l}\sim q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{t}^{UM})}\log\mu_{\theta}^{l}(x_{t},t)[x_{0}^{l}]\,\mathrm{d}t = ∫ 0 + ∞ − α t 1 − α t 𝔼 x t ∑ l : x t l = [ MASK ] ∑ a ≠ [ MASK ] q 0 l ( a | x t U ​ M ) log μ θ l ( x t , t ) [ a ] d t . \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}-\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{t}}\sum_{l:x_{t}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}\sum_{a\neq\mathrm{[MASK]}}q_{0}^{l}(a|x_{t}^{UM})\log\mu_{\theta}^{l}(x_{t},t)[a]\,\mathrm{d}t.

[289] p: This establishes ( 25 ).

[290] p: Second, we show the second term in ( 24 ) satisfies:

[291] table: ∫ 0 + ∞ α t 1 − α t 𝔼 x t ∑ l : x t l = [ MASK ] H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x t U ​ M ) ) d t = ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) , \int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{t}}\sum_{l:x_{t}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{t}^{UM})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right), (26)

[292] p: where ℳ k ⊆ [ d ] \mathcal{M}_{k}\subseteq[d] , with | ℳ k | = k |\mathcal{M}_{k}|=k for k = 1 , … , d k=1,\ldots,d , denotes the subset with exactly k k token indices uniformly sampled from the total index set, ℳ k c = [ d ] ∖ ℳ k \mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}=[d]\setminus\mathcal{M}_{k} , x 0 ℳ k c := { x 0 l } l ∈ ℳ k c x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}}:=\{x_{0}^{l}\}_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}} , and q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}}) denotes the conditional distribution of x 0 l x_{0}^{l} given x 0 ℳ k c x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}} .

[293] p: We proceed as follows.

[294] table: ∫ 0 + ∞ \displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty} α t 1 − α t 𝔼 x t ∑ l : x t l = [ MASK ] H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x t U ​ M ) ) d t \displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{t}}\sum_{l:x_{t}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{t}^{UM})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t = ∫ 0 + ∞ α t 1 − α t 𝔼 k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) d t \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t = ∫ 0 + ∞ α t 1 − α t ∑ k = 1 d ( d k ) ( 1 − α t ) k α t d − k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) d t \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\binom{d}{k}(1-\alpha_{t})^{k}\alpha_{t}^{d-k}\ \mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t = ∫ 0 1 1 1 − α t ∑ k = 1 d ( d k ) ( 1 − α t ) k α t d − k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) d α t \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{1-\alpha_{t}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\binom{d}{k}(1-\alpha_{t})^{k}\alpha_{t}^{d-k}\ \mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right)\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{t} = ∫ 0 1 ∑ k = 1 d d ! k ! ​ ( d − k ) ! ( 1 − α t ) k − 1 α t d − k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) d α t \displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{d!}{k!(d-k)!}(1-\alpha_{t})^{k-1}\alpha_{t}^{d-k}\ \mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right)\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{t} = ∑ k = 1 d 1 k ( ∫ 0 1 d ! ( k − 1 ) ! ​ ( d − k ) ! ( 1 − α t ) k − 1 α t d − k d α t ) 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d!}{(k-1)!(d-k)!}(1-\alpha_{t})^{k-1}\alpha_{t}^{d-k}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{t}\right)\ \mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right) = ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) . \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right).

[295] p: This establishes ( 26 ).

[296] p: Finally, combining ( 25 ) and ( 26 ), we obtain as desired,

[297] table: ∫ 0 + ∞ ℒ S ​ E ​ ( s , t ) ​ 𝑑 t \displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathcal{L}_{SE}(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}t = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 [ ℒ ∞ ( x 0 ) ] − ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) . \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})]-\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right). (27)

[298] p: The proof is now complete.

[299] h2: Appendix I FHS Sampling in CTMC Framework: Proposition 3 and its Proof

[300] p: In this section, we establish Proposition 3 , which provides statistical characterization of the FHS sampling process and show that it exactly matches the reverse process under the CTMC framework for absorbing-rate diffusion models. This will highly facilitate us to develop the error analysis for Theorem 4 .

[301] p: We let ℙ FHS \mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}} and ℙ CTMC \mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}} denote the probability measure over the entire path generated by the FHS sampling algorithm and the ground truth reverse absorbing-rate continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC). Both processes admit a common simple representation. Since the sequence contains d d tokens, starts from the fully masked state, and each token remains fixed once it is unmasked, both processes are fully characterized by: (i) the d d unmasking times, (ii) which token is unmasked at each such time, and (iii) the token value to which that token is unmasked. We therefore introduce notation for these random variables. Let k ∈ 0 , 1 , … , d k\in{0,1,\ldots,d} index the unmasking events.

[302] p: For k ∈ [ d ] k\in[d] , let τ k \tau_{k} denote the time at which the ( d − k ) (d-k) -th unmasking occurs. Specifically, τ d \tau_{d} denotes the initialization time of the sampling processes. Clearly, unmasking times { τ k } k \{\tau_{k}\}_{k} are increasing monotonically with respect to k k , satisfying

[303] table: τ 0 < τ 1 < ⋯ < τ d − 1 < τ d = + ∞ , \tau_{0}<\tau_{1}<\cdots<\tau_{d-1}<\tau_{d}=+\infty,

[304] p: where τ d = + ∞ \tau_{d}=+\infty is initialized by α ⁡ ( τ d ) = e − τ d = 0 \alpha(\tau_{d})=e^{-\tau_{d}}=0 .

[305] p: Let x τ k x_{\tau_{k}} denote the sequence state immediately after time τ k \tau_{k} , i.e., τ k − δ \tau_{k}-\delta . Between consecutive unmasking events, the state remains constant; specifically, we set x t ≡ x τ k x_{t}\equiv x_{\tau_{k}} for t ∈ ( τ k − 1 , τ k ] t\in(\tau_{k-1},\tau_{k}] (equivalently, until the next unmasking time).

[306] p: Let i τ k ∈ [ d ] i_{\tau_{k}}\in[d] denote the token index unmasked at time τ k \tau_{k} .

[307] p: Let a τ k ∈ [ S ] a_{\tau_{k}}\in[S] denote the realized token value assigned at that time. Thus, the newly revealed token satisfies x τ k i τ k = a τ k x_{\tau_{k}}^{i_{\tau_{k}}}=a_{\tau_{k}} .

[308] p: To simplify the notation, we use x k , i k , a k x_{k},i_{k},a_{k} to denote x τ k , i τ k , a τ k x_{\tau_{k}},i_{\tau_{k}},a_{\tau_{k}} , respectively. Further note that x k x_{k} is fully determined by { i j } j = d k \{i_{j}\}_{j=d}^{k} and { a j } j = d k \{a_{j}\}_{j=d}^{k} .

[309] p: The following proposition provides useful statistical properties of the FHS and reverse CTMC processes and their connections.

[310] h6: Proposition 3 .

[311] p: The FHS and reverse absorbing-rate CTMC processes satisfy the following properties:

[312] table: ℙ FHS ​ ( τ k − 1 | x k , τ k ) = ℙ CTMC ​ ( τ k − 1 | x k , τ k ) = ℙ CTMC ​ ( τ k − 1 | τ k ) , \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}}(\tau_{k-1}|x_{k},\tau_{k})=\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}}(\tau_{k-1}|x_{k},\tau_{k})=\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}}(\tau_{k-1}|\tau_{k}), ℙ FHS ​ ( i k − 1 | x k , τ k − 1 ) = ℙ CTMC ​ ( i k − 1 | x k , τ k − 1 ) = 1 k , \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}}(i_{k-1}|x_{k},\tau_{k-1})=\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}}(i_{k-1}|x_{k},\tau_{k-1})=\frac{1}{k}, ℙ FHS ​ ( a k − 1 | x k , τ k − 1 , i k − 1 ) = μ θ i k − 1 ​ ( x k , τ k − 1 ) ​ [ a k − 1 ] , \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}}(a_{k-1}|x_{k},\tau_{k-1},i_{k-1})=\mu_{\theta}^{i_{k-1}}(x_{k},\tau_{k-1})[a_{k-1}], ℙ CTMC ​ ( a k − 1 | x k , τ k − 1 , i k − 1 ) = q 0 i k − 1 ​ ( a k − 1 | x k U ​ M ) . \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}}(a_{k-1}|x_{k},\tau_{k-1},i_{k-1})=q_{0}^{i_{k-1}}(a_{k-1}|x_{k}^{UM}).

[313] p: Clearly, the second equality of the first property shows that given τ k \tau_{k} , the next umasking time τ k − 1 \tau_{k-1} is independent of x k x_{k} .

[314] h6: Proof.

[315] p: The proof contains three steps: Step 1 shows the basic properties of transition dynamics of reverse CTMC, Step 2 further develops the statistical distributions for reverse CTMC, and Step 3 establishes the connection between the statistics of FHS and reverse CTMC processes.

[316] p: Step 1. Basic properties of reverse CTMC: We use R ~ t \widetilde{R}_{t} to denote the rate matrix of the reverse absorbing-rate CTMC indexed by forward time t t . This is for the convenience to analyze both processes with the same time indexing. Specifically, we define the reverse rate matrix R ~ t \widetilde{R}_{t} indexed by the forward time t t as, for y ≠ x y\neq x ,

[317] table: R ~ t ​ ( x , y ) := lim δ ↓ 0 ℙ ⁡ ( X t − δ = y | X t = x ) δ , \widetilde{R}_{t}(x,y)\;:=\;\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{P}\!\left(X_{t-\delta}=y\,\middle|\,X_{t}=x\right)}{\delta}, (28)

[318] p: or equivalently,

[319] table: ℙ ( X t − Δ ​ t = y | X t = x ) = 𝟙 { y = x } + R ~ t ( x , y ) Δ t + o ( Δ t ) . \mathbb{P}(X_{t-\Delta t}=y|X_{t}=x)=\mathds{1}\left\{y=x\right\}+\widetilde{R}_{t}(x,y)\Delta t+o(\Delta t).

[320] p: The diagonal entries are defined by

[321] table: R ~ s ( x , x ) = − ∑ y ≠ x R ~ s ( x , y ) . \widetilde{R}_{s}(x,x)\;=\;-\sum_{y\neq x}\widetilde{R}_{s}(x,y).

[322] p: When horizon T T is finite, then the defined reverse rate matrix R ~ t \widetilde{R}_{t} indexed by the forward time is equivalent to previous reverse rate matrix R → t \mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle R\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle R\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle R\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle R\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{t} by

[323] table: R ~ t ​ ( x , y ) = R → T − t ​ ( x , y ) , ∀ y ≠ x . \widetilde{R}_{t}(x,y)=\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle R\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle R\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle R\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle R\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{T-t}(x,y),\quad\forall y\neq x.

[324] p: Based on the notations and definitions above, we next have several basic results concerning time-inhomogeneous CTMCs in Lemma 5 .

[325] h6: Lemma 5 .

[326] p: Let ( Y t ) t (Y_{t})_{t} denote the reverse absorbing-rate time-inhomogeneous CTMC with the generator matrix { R ~ t } \{\widetilde{R}_{t}\} defined in ( 28 ). Let the unmasking time in state x x be τ := sup { s < t : Y s ≠ x } \tau:=\sup\{s<t:Y_{s}\neq x\} . Then

[327] p: The instantaneous total rate of leaving x x at time t t is

[328] table: Λ ⁡ ( t , x ) = ∑ y ≠ x R ~ t ​ ( x , y ) . \Lambda(t,x)=\sum_{y\neq x}\widetilde{R}_{t}(x,y).

[329] p: The unmasking time τ \tau in state x x satisfies

[330] table: ℙ ( τ < t − h ∣ Y t = x ) = exp ( − ∫ t − h t Λ ( u , x ) d u ) . \mathbb{P}(\tau<t-h\mid Y_{t}=x)=\exp\!\left(-\int_{t-h}^{t}\Lambda(u,x)\,du\right).

[331] p: Given that unmasking occurs at time τ \tau , the distribution of the predicted token value satisfies

[332] table: lim δ ↓ 0 ℙ ( Y τ = y ∣ Y t = x , τ ∈ ( t − δ , t ] ) = R ~ t ​ ( x , y ) Λ ⁡ ( t , x ) . \lim\limits_{\delta\downarrow 0}\ \mathbb{P}(Y_{\tau}=y\mid Y_{t}=x,\tau\in(t-\delta,t])=\frac{\widetilde{R}_{t}(x,y)}{\Lambda(t,x)}.

[333] p: The full proof of Lemma 5 is provided in Section L.5 .

[334] p: Step 2. Distributions induced by ℙ CTMC \mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}} : We characterize some important distributions induced by ℙ CTMC \mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}} , which will be useful to establish the connection between ℙ FHS \mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}} and ℙ CTMC \mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}} . For any x k x_{k} with k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , there must exist k k [ MASK ] \mathrm{[MASK]} tokens in x k x_{k} due to the definition of x k x_{k} . Then without loss of generality, let x i x^{i} be one of the [ MASK ] \mathrm{[MASK]} tokens in the following proof.

[335] p: Consider the reverse rate matrix R ~ t \widetilde{R}_{t} with the forward time index t t of CTMC. For any a ≠ [ MASK ] a\neq\mathrm{[MASK]} , we have

[336] table: R ~ t ( x , x − i ⊕ i a ) = R t ( x − i ⊕ i a , x ) ⋅ q t ( x − i ⊕ i a ) q t ​ ( x ) = q t ( x − i ⊕ i a ) q t ​ ( x ) . \widetilde{R}_{t}\big(x,x^{-i}\oplus_{i}a\big)=R_{t}\big(x^{-i}\oplus_{i}a,x\big)\cdot\frac{q_{t}(x^{-i}\oplus_{i}a)}{q_{t}(x)}=\frac{q_{t}(x^{-i}\oplus_{i}a)}{q_{t}(x)}. (29)

[337] p: Following from Claim 1 of Lemma 5 , the total rate of leaving x x at time t t on index i i is given by

[338] table: r i ( t , x ) := ∑ b ≠ [ MASK ] R ~ t ( x , x − i ⊕ i b ) = ∑ b ≠ [ MASK ] q t ( x − i ⊕ i b ) q t ​ ( x ) = α t 1 − α t , r_{i}(t,x):=\sum_{b\neq\mathrm{[MASK]}}\widetilde{R}_{t}(x,x^{-i}\oplus_{i}b)=\frac{\sum_{b\neq\mathrm{[MASK]}}q_{t}(x^{-i}\oplus_{i}b)}{q_{t}(x)}=\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}, (30)

[339] p: where the last equality follows from ( 23 ). Then the total reverse jump rate of leaving x x at time t t is given by

[340] table: Λ ( t , x ) = ∑ y ≠ x R ~ t ( x , y ) = ∑ i : x i = [ MASK ] r i ( t , x ) = m ( x ) ⋅ α t 1 − α t , \Lambda(t,x)=\sum_{y\neq x}\widetilde{R}_{t}(x,y)=\sum_{i:x^{i}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}r_{i}(t,x)=m(x)\cdot\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}, (31)

[341] p: where m ⁡ ( x ) m(x) denotes the number of masked tokens in x x .

[342] p: Then, substituting ( 31 ) into Claim 2 of Lemma 5 , we can derive the conditional distribution of τ k − 1 \tau_{k-1} given x k x_{k} and τ k \tau_{k} as:

[343] table: ℙ CTMC ​ ( τ k − 1 < t | x k , τ k ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}}(\tau_{k-1}<t|x_{k},\tau_{k}) = exp ( − ∫ t τ k Λ ( u , x k ) d u ) \displaystyle=\exp{\left(-\int_{t}^{\tau_{k}}\Lambda(u,x_{k})\mathrm{d}u\right)} = exp ( − ∫ t τ k k ⋅ e − u 1 − e − u d u ) \displaystyle=\exp\!\Big(-\!\!\int_{t}^{\tau_{k}}k\cdot\frac{e^{-u}}{1-e^{-u}}\,du\Big) = exp ( − k ⋅ log ( 1 − e − u ) | u = t τ k ) \displaystyle=\exp\!\Big(-\!\!k\cdot\log(1-e^{-u})|_{u=t}^{\tau_{k}}\Big) = ( 1 − e − t 1 − e − τ k ) k , \displaystyle=\left(\frac{1-e^{-t}}{1-e^{-\tau_{k}}}\right)^{k},

[344] p: which we note that τ k − 1 \tau_{k-1} does not depend on x k x_{k} given τ k \tau_{k} .

[345] p: Next, we derive ℙ CTMC ​ ( i k − 1 | x k , τ k − 1 ) \mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}}(i_{k-1}|x_{k},\tau_{k-1}) . Applying claim 3 in Lemma 5 together with ( 30 ) and ( 31 ), we obtain

[346] table: ℙ CTMC ​ ( i k − 1 | x k , τ k − 1 ) \displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}}(i_{k-1}|x_{k},\tau_{k-1}) = ∑ b ≠ [ MASK ] R ~ τ k − 1 ( x k , x k − i k − 1 ⊕ i k − 1 b ) Λ ⁡ ( τ k − 1 , x k ) \displaystyle=\sum_{b\neq\mathrm{[MASK]}}\frac{\widetilde{R}_{\tau_{k-1}}(x_{k},x_{k}^{-i_{k-1}}\oplus_{i_{k-1}}b)}{\Lambda(\tau_{k-1},x_{k})} = r i k − 1 ​ ( τ k − 1 , x k ) Λ ⁡ ( τ k − 1 , x k ) = α t 1 − α t k ⋅ α t 1 − α t = 1 k . \displaystyle=\frac{r_{i_{k-1}}(\tau_{k-1},x_{k})}{\Lambda(\tau_{k-1},x_{k})}=\frac{\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}}{k\cdot\frac{\alpha_{t}}{1-\alpha_{t}}}=\frac{1}{k}.

[347] p: Finally, we derive the conditional distribution over the predicted token value a a . Combining ( 30 ) and Claim 3 in Lemma 5 , we derive, for all t t and i i ,

[366] h2: Appendix J Proof of Theorem 4

[367] p: As shown by Proposition 3 , the FHS sampling process and the reverse CTMC decoupling structure: they share the same statistical distributions on the unmasking times τ k \tau_{k} and the indices i k i_{k} of tokens that are unmasked, and differ only in the mechanism used to sample the predicted token value a k a_{k} for each k k . In particular, FHS predicts a k − 1 a_{k-1} via μ θ i k − 1 \mu_{\theta}^{i_{k-1}} as an approximation of q 0 i k − 1 ​ ( a | x k U ​ M ) q_{0}^{i_{k-1}}(a|x_{k}^{UM}) used in the ground-truth reverse CTMC. Thus, the estimation error in μ θ \mu_{\theta} affects only the sampling of the token value a k a_{k} and does not propagate to τ k \tau_{k} or i k i_{k} . As a consequence, the subsequent convergence analysis of FHS can be carried out by technically accumulating only the errors arising from sampling the predicted token value a k − 1 a_{k-1} for k = 1 , 2 , … , d k={1,2,\ldots,d} .

[368] p: In the following, we first derive the KL divergence between the path distributions of the two processes, i.e., KL ( ℙ CTMC ( path ) | | ℙ FHS ( path ) ) \mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}}(\mathrm{path})||\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}}(\mathrm{path})) . Then, by applying the data processing inequality, we obtain the error bound on the distance between the ground-truth distribution q 0 ​ ( ⋅ ) q_{0}(\cdot) and the output distribution of FHS, i.e., KL ( q 0 ( ⋅ ) | | ℙ FHS ( ⋅ ) ) \mathrm{KL}(q_{0}(\cdot)||\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}}(\cdot)) .

[400] table: 𝔼 x 0 ​ [ ℒ ∞ ​ ( x 0 ) ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}\left[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}\left(x_{0}\right)\right] (38) = 𝔼 x 0 ∑ k = 1 d 𝔼 x k ∼ q ~ k | 0 ​ ( x k ∣ x 0 ) 1 k ∑ l : x k l = [ MASK ] ( − log μ ¯ θ ( x k ) [ x 0 l ] ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{x_{k}\sim\tilde{q}_{k|0}\left(x_{k}\mid x_{0}\right)}\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l:x_{k}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}(-\log\bar{\mu}_{\theta}\left(x_{k}\right)\left[x_{0}^{l}\right]) = ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x 0 , x k ∑ l : x k l = [ MASK ] ( − log μ ¯ θ ( x k ) [ x 0 l ] ) . \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0},x_{k}}\sum_{l:x_{k}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}(-\log\bar{\mu}_{\theta}\left(x_{k}\right)\left[x_{0}^{l}\right]). = ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x k 𝔼 x 0 | x k ∑ l : x k l = [ MASK ] ( − log μ ¯ θ ( x k ) [ x 0 l ] ) . \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\mid x_{k}}\sum_{l:x_{k}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}(-\log\bar{\mu}_{\theta}\left(x_{k}\right)\left[x_{0}^{l}\right]). = ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x k ∑ l : x k l = [ MASK ] 𝔼 x 0 | x k [ − log μ ¯ θ ( x k ) [ x 0 l ] ] . \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{k}}\sum_{l:x_{k}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\mid x_{k}}\left[-\log\bar{\mu}_{\theta}\left(x_{k}\right)\left[x_{0}^{l}\right]\right]. = ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x k ∑ l : x k l = [ MASK ] 𝔼 x 0 l | x k [ − log μ ¯ θ ( x k ) [ x 0 l ] ] . \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{k}}\sum_{l:x_{k}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}^{l}\mid x_{k}}\left[-\log\bar{\mu}_{\theta}\left(x_{k}\right)\left[x_{0}^{l}\right]\right]. = ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x k ∑ l : x k l = [ MASK ] 𝔼 a ∼ q 0 l ( ⋅ | x k U ​ M ) [ − log μ ¯ θ ( x k ) [ a ] ] , \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{k}}\sum_{l:x_{k}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{k}^{UM})}\left[-\log\bar{\mu}_{\theta}\left(x_{k}\right)\left[a\right]\right],

[401] p: which is precisely the first term in ( 37 ).

[402] p: Finally, combining ( 34 ), ( 37 ), and ( 38 ), we have

[403] table: KL ( ℙ CTMC ( path ) | | ℙ FHS ( path ) ) \displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{CTMC}}(\mathrm{path})||\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{FHS}}(\mathrm{path})) = − ∑ k = 1 d 𝔼 x k 1 k ∑ l : x k l = [ MASK ] 𝔼 a ∼ q 0 l ( ⋅ | x k U ​ M ) [ log ( μ ¯ θ l ( x k ) [ a ] ) ] \displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{x_{k}}\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l:x_{k}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}\mathbb{E}_{a\sim q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{k}^{UM})}\left[\log(\bar{\mu}_{\theta}^{l}(x_{k})[a])\right] − ∑ k = 1 d 𝔼 x k 1 k ∑ l : x k l = [ MASK ] H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x k U ​ M ) ) \displaystyle-\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{x_{k}}\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l:x_{k}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}H(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{k}^{UM})) = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 [ ℒ ∞ ( x 0 ) ] − ∑ k = 1 d 𝔼 x k 1 k ∑ l : x k l = [ MASK ] H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x k U ​ M ) ) \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\left[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})\right]-\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{x_{k}}\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l:x_{k}^{l}=\mathrm{[MASK]}}H(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{k}^{UM})) = 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 [ ℒ ∞ ( x 0 ) ] − ∑ k = 1 d 1 k 𝔼 x 0 ∼ q 0 𝔼 ℳ k ∑ l ∈ ℳ k H ( q 0 l ( ⋅ | x 0 ℳ k c ) ) . \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\left[\mathcal{L}_{\infty}(x_{0})\right]-\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{E}_{x_{0}\sim q_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{M}_{k}}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{M}_{k}}H\left(q_{0}^{l}(\cdot|x_{0}^{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{c}})\right).
